Grounding labeling, round 2 (blind)

saves labels_3b_<name>.csv · progress autosaves in this browser
How to label (read once)
Label alone and do not discuss labels with the others until all three files are in. Your first labels are final.
Each case shows an incident description (INPUT) and the evidence a diagnosis system collected, then 2 claims from its explanation. Judge each claim against the evidence on this page only (closed set: no outside knowledge, plausibility does not count).
Supported: the evidence directly states what the claim asserts; it follows from the evidence alone.
Partially Supported: the evidence has related information, but the claim needs inference, extrapolation, or domain knowledge not in the evidence.
Unsupported: the evidence contradicts the claim (different number, service, metric, or direction), or nothing in the evidence is about it.
Compare numbers, service names, metric names and times exactly; a value counts only for the service it is listed under. Root-cause / fault-category / indicator claims: Supported if the evidence clearly singles out what the claim names; Unsupported if it clearly singles out something else or the named metric does not appear; Partially only if the evidence is mixed. A generic caveat in a tool output (such as "not causal proof") does not by itself lower the label.
Evidence IDs: the items your label rests on (INPUT for the case input). Leave empty for Unsupported-because-absent. Click an ID on the left to add it to the selected claim.
When done: type your name at the top, press Download CSV, and send the file back.

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f10-wrong-contacts-api.md] (score=0.56) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F10-01: Reservation Flow Calls the Wrong Contacts API
- **Affected Services**: ts-preserve-service, ts-contacts-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: A specific contact type or reservation path fails with HTTP 400, 404, 405, or 500 while ordinary reservations succeed; `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}` is concentrated in one request subtype; traces show an unexpected contacts endpoint or response schema
- **Root Cause**: The F10 branch-selection logic in `ts-preserve-service` maps an edge-case reservation to the wrong `ts-contacts-service` operation. The called endpoint expects different parameters or semantics, so its response cannot be used by the reservation workflow
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Break down preserve failures by contact type and booking path; if custom instrumentation exports `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}`, graph `rate(reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}[5m])`, otherwise derive the cohort from structured preserve-service error logs and compare it with total preserve requests
2. Diff successful and failed traces at the contacts call, including route template, method, sanitized request shape, response status, and deployed versions; correlate the failing branch with preserve-service logs
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-preserve-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` and `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-contacts-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` to verify the selected endpoint and rejection reason
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — route the affected scenario to the correct contacts operation or disable only that path while preserving standard reservations; roll back if the mapping changed in the current release. Long-term — use a typed client with one explicit discriminator, validate inputs before dispatch, add consumer-driven contract tests for every branch, and include the edge case in end-to-end reservation tests

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-basic-info-thread-pool.md] (score=0.54) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-01: Basic Info Thread Pool Exhaustion
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-travel-service, ts-ticketinfo-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Ticket-information requests slow down or return HTTP 504 errors during burst traffic; `jvm_threads_live_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` and the application worker-pool active count reach their configured maxima; executor queue depth rises while pod CPU can remain below its limit; traces show long gaps before work begins in `ts-basic-service`
- **Root Cause**: The F5 asynchronous ticket-information path submits blocking dependency calls to a bounded shared executor. Slow tasks occupy every worker, new tasks accumulate in the queue or are rejected, and callers time out before the service can schedule their work
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `executor_active_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` with `executor_pool_max_threads` and graph `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-basic-service"}` alongside `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-basic-service",status=~"5.."}[5m])`
2. Inspect a failed distributed trace from `ts-travel-service` through `ts-basic-service`; confirm that the server span has high scheduling or queue wait and correlate it with `RejectedExecutionException` or `TimeoutException` in the same trace ID
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump to container logs, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'RUNNABLE|WAITING|BLOCKED|RejectedExecutionException|TimeoutException'`; use an approved JDK diagnostic container only if `SIGQUIT` dumps are disabled
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — shed nonessential ticket-info traffic, open a circuit breaker for the slow dependency, and scale only after confirming downstream capacity; if a release changed executor behavior, roll it back. Long-term — isolate blocking dependencies in separate bounded pools, enforce per-call deadlines and queue limits, add back-pressure or fallback responses, size executors from measured concurrency, and load-test the F5 path

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.54) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f6-sql-retry-storm.md] (score=0.53) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F6-01: Voucher SQL Error Triggers Recursive Retry Storm
- **Affected Services**: ts-voucher-service, ts-order-service, ts-order-other-service, ts-ui-dashboard
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Voucher operations first return SQL errors and then degrade into HTTP 504 failures; request rate to `ts-voucher-service`, retry count, database connection usage, and executor queue depth rise without a matching increase in user traffic; traces contain repeated nested calls for the same order
- **Root Cause**: The F6 voucher path executes an invalid SQL statement or argument. The voucher-request caller retries this nonrecoverable failure without a strict attempt budget, multiplying one user request into repeated voucher calls and exhausting the voucher service or its MySQL connection pool; `ts-voucher-service` may call `ts-order-service` or `ts-order-other-service` to retrieve the referenced order, not the reverse
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `rate(sql_error_total{service="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, `rate(retry_attempts_total{target="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, and the voucher MySQL pool's active and pending connection metrics
2. Inspect one voucher trace by `order_id`: confirm repeated caller-to-voucher attempts, then identify the first voucher SQL error before secondary timeouts or pool-exhaustion errors; verify whether the voucher lookup called `ts-order-service` or `ts-order-other-service`
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-voucher-service --since=10m | grep -E 'SQL|OperationalError|ProgrammingError|timeout'` and inspect the identified caller's logs for the same `order_id` with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/<caller-service> --since=10m | grep -E '<order-id>|retry|voucher'`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — stop retries at the identified caller with a circuit breaker or feature flag, roll back or disable the faulty voucher query, and let the backlog and MySQL pool recover before restoring traffic. Long-term — classify SQL syntax and schema errors as nonretryable, cap attempts with jittered backoff and a retry budget, add voucher-query integration tests against the deployed schema, and alert when retry amplification exceeds original request volume

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f14-second-class-price.md] (score=0.51) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F14-01: Incorrect Second-Class Ticket Price Calculation
- **Affected Services**: ts-price-service, ts-basic-service, ts-ticketinfo-service, ts-travel-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Second-class quotes differ from the expected fare table while first-class results remain correct; `ticket_price_calculation_error_total{seat_class="second"}` or price-deviation alerts rise without HTTP failures; order traces carry an internally consistent but incorrect amount
- **Root Cause**: The F14 second-class calculation branch applies an incorrect arithmetic rule, constant, multiplier, or field mapping while producing a syntactically valid amount. Normal availability checks therefore do not detect the business-logic error
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare second-class price distributions with first-class and a versioned fare-table baseline; if custom instrumentation exports `ticket_price_calculation_error_total{seat_class="second"}`, use it as supporting evidence, otherwise derive the mismatch count from structured quote-validation logs; identify the first release with deviation
2. Inspect a quote trace for route, seat class, base fare, multiplier, discount inputs, and final amount; replay the same sanitized inputs through the known-good formula and compare each calculation stage
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-price-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` and, from an approved debug pod, call `http://ts-price-service:16579/api/v1/priceservice/prices/<route-id>/<train-type>` for a controlled route to compare the returned second-class fare with the approved fixture
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — disable sale of affected second-class quotes or roll back the pricing release, recalculate impacted unpaid orders, and follow the approved correction process for completed purchases. Long-term — centralize fare rules, use table-driven unit and property tests across classes and discounts, version rule inputs, add price sanity bounds, and require golden-fixture comparison before deployment

Claims to label

claim C02a
An increased number of requests being blocked or timed out as a result of the error in `ts-preserve-service` could lead to a degraded user experience.
claim C02b
Evidence E001 details TT-CODE-F10-01, which implicates `ts-preserve-service` in a specific branch-selection logic error.

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f10-wrong-contacts-api.md] (score=0.56) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F10-01: Reservation Flow Calls the Wrong Contacts API
- **Affected Services**: ts-preserve-service, ts-contacts-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: A specific contact type or reservation path fails with HTTP 400, 404, 405, or 500 while ordinary reservations succeed; `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}` is concentrated in one request subtype; traces show an unexpected contacts endpoint or response schema
- **Root Cause**: The F10 branch-selection logic in `ts-preserve-service` maps an edge-case reservation to the wrong `ts-contacts-service` operation. The called endpoint expects different parameters or semantics, so its response cannot be used by the reservation workflow
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Break down preserve failures by contact type and booking path; if custom instrumentation exports `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}`, graph `rate(reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}[5m])`, otherwise derive the cohort from structured preserve-service error logs and compare it with total preserve requests
2. Diff successful and failed traces at the contacts call, including route template, method, sanitized request shape, response status, and deployed versions; correlate the failing branch with preserve-service logs
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-preserve-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` and `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-contacts-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` to verify the selected endpoint and rejection reason
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — route the affected scenario to the correct contacts operation or disable only that path while preserving standard reservations; roll back if the mapping changed in the current release. Long-term — use a typed client with one explicit discriminator, validate inputs before dispatch, add consumer-driven contract tests for every branch, and include the edge case in end-to-end reservation tests

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.54) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-basic-info-thread-pool.md] (score=0.54) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-01: Basic Info Thread Pool Exhaustion
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-travel-service, ts-ticketinfo-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Ticket-information requests slow down or return HTTP 504 errors during burst traffic; `jvm_threads_live_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` and the application worker-pool active count reach their configured maxima; executor queue depth rises while pod CPU can remain below its limit; traces show long gaps before work begins in `ts-basic-service`
- **Root Cause**: The F5 asynchronous ticket-information path submits blocking dependency calls to a bounded shared executor. Slow tasks occupy every worker, new tasks accumulate in the queue or are rejected, and callers time out before the service can schedule their work
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `executor_active_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` with `executor_pool_max_threads` and graph `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-basic-service"}` alongside `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-basic-service",status=~"5.."}[5m])`
2. Inspect a failed distributed trace from `ts-travel-service` through `ts-basic-service`; confirm that the server span has high scheduling or queue wait and correlate it with `RejectedExecutionException` or `TimeoutException` in the same trace ID
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump to container logs, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'RUNNABLE|WAITING|BLOCKED|RejectedExecutionException|TimeoutException'`; use an approved JDK diagnostic container only if `SIGQUIT` dumps are disabled
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — shed nonessential ticket-info traffic, open a circuit breaker for the slow dependency, and scale only after confirming downstream capacity; if a release changed executor behavior, roll it back. Long-term — isolate blocking dependencies in separate bounded pools, enforce per-call deadlines and queue limits, add back-pressure or fallback responses, size executors from measured concurrency, and load-test the F5 path

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f6-sql-retry-storm.md] (score=0.54) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F6-01: Voucher SQL Error Triggers Recursive Retry Storm
- **Affected Services**: ts-voucher-service, ts-order-service, ts-order-other-service, ts-ui-dashboard
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Voucher operations first return SQL errors and then degrade into HTTP 504 failures; request rate to `ts-voucher-service`, retry count, database connection usage, and executor queue depth rise without a matching increase in user traffic; traces contain repeated nested calls for the same order
- **Root Cause**: The F6 voucher path executes an invalid SQL statement or argument. The voucher-request caller retries this nonrecoverable failure without a strict attempt budget, multiplying one user request into repeated voucher calls and exhausting the voucher service or its MySQL connection pool; `ts-voucher-service` may call `ts-order-service` or `ts-order-other-service` to retrieve the referenced order, not the reverse
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `rate(sql_error_total{service="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, `rate(retry_attempts_total{target="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-voucher-service"}[5m])`, and the voucher MySQL pool's active and pending connection metrics
2. Inspect one voucher trace by `order_id`: confirm repeated caller-to-voucher attempts, then identify the first voucher SQL error before secondary timeouts or pool-exhaustion errors; verify whether the voucher lookup called `ts-order-service` or `ts-order-other-service`
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-voucher-service --since=10m | grep -E 'SQL|OperationalError|ProgrammingError|timeout'` and inspect the identified caller's logs for the same `order_id` with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/<caller-service> --since=10m | grep -E '<order-id>|retry|voucher'`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — stop retries at the identified caller with a circuit breaker or feature flag, roll back or disable the faulty voucher query, and let the backlog and MySQL pool recover before restoring traffic. Long-term — classify SQL syntax and schema errors as nonretryable, cap attempts with jittered backoff and a retry budget, add voucher-query integration tests against the deployed schema, and alert when retry amplification exceeds original request volume

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.55) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-basic-info-thread-pool.md] (score=0.47) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-01: Basic Info Thread Pool Exhaustion
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-travel-service, ts-ticketinfo-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Ticket-information requests slow down or return HTTP 504 errors during burst traffic; `jvm_threads_live_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` and the application worker-pool active count reach their configured maxima; executor queue depth rises while pod CPU can remain below its limit; traces show long gaps before work begins in `ts-basic-service`
- **Root Cause**: The F5 asynchronous ticket-information path submits blocking dependency calls to a bounded shared executor. Slow tasks occupy every worker, new tasks accumulate in the queue or are rejected, and callers time out before the service can schedule their work
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `executor_active_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` with `executor_pool_max_threads` and graph `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-basic-service"}` alongside `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-basic-service",status=~"5.."}[5m])`
2. Inspect a failed distributed trace from `ts-travel-service` through `ts-basic-service`; confirm that the server span has high scheduling or queue wait and correlate it with `RejectedExecutionException` or `TimeoutException` in the same trace ID
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump to container logs, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'RUNNABLE|WAITING|BLOCKED|RejectedExecutionException|TimeoutException'`; use an approved JDK diagnostic container only if `SIGQUIT` dumps are disabled
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — shed nonessential ticket-info traffic, open a circuit breaker for the slow dependency, and scale only after confirming downstream capacity; if a release changed executor behavior, roll it back. Long-term — isolate blocking dependencies in separate bounded pools, enforce per-call deadlines and queue limits, add back-pressure or fallback responses, size executors from measured concurrency, and load-test the F5 path

[synthesized/01_sock-shop_resource_orders-cpu-hog.md] (score=0.41) # Sock Shop Resource Faults

# Sock Shop Resource Faults  
## SS-RES-CPU-001: Orders Service CPU Hogging
- **Affected Services**: orders
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Java orders pods sustain CPU usage near their cgroup limit; order submission p95/p99 latency rises; HTTP 502/503/504 responses may propagate through front-end; CFS throttling and runnable application threads increase while catalogue, carts, orders-db, payment, and shipping may remain healthy
- **Root Cause**: A tight loop, runaway serialization, inefficient total calculation, or unbounded processing of an oversized request consumes the orders container's CPU. Cgroup throttling then delays HTTP handlers, MongoDB operations, and downstream calls, causing queueing and timeouts even though the pod remains running. The precise hot path must be established from repeated stack or profile evidence
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod=~"orders-.*",container="orders"}[5m]))` with `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total{pod=~"orders-.*",container="orders"}[5m])) / sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_periods_total{pod=~"orders-.*",container="orders"}[5m]))`; correlate saturation with `sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{pod=~"orders-.*"}[5m]))` rather than assuming legitimate load
2. Inspect a slow front-end-to-orders trace and separate orders self-time from MongoDB or downstream wait time; review `kubectl logs deployment/orders -n <namespace> --since=20m` for timeouts, GC pressure, and the affected request path, while avoiding customer payloads or credentials
3. Run `kubectl top pods -n <namespace> -l name=orders --containers` and inspect limits with `kubectl get deployment orders -n <namespace> -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[?(@.name=="orders")].resources}'`. Because the orders image is JRE-based, do not assume `jcmd` exists in it; when authorized, attach a compatible JDK ephemeral debug container with `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<orders-pod> -it --image=eclipse-temurin:8-jdk --target=orders`, identify the actual orders Java PID, and collect several `jcmd <java-pid> Thread.print` samples to find persistently runnable stacks
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the hot pod from traffic, preserve repeated thread evidence, roll back or feature-disable the correlated path, and scale only known-good replicas if orders-db and downstream services have headroom. Long-term — prof...

Evidence

[synthesized/05_sock-shop_network_rabbitmq-amqp-failure.md] (score=0.51) # Sock Shop Network Faults

# Sock Shop Network Faults  
## SS-NET-AMQP-001: RabbitMQ Connectivity Failure Disrupts Shipping Tasks
- **Affected Services**: shipping, queue-master, rabbitmq
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: If shipping cannot publish, its HTTP endpoint can still accept the shipment while logging that it could not add the task to the queue, so no RabbitMQ backlog is guaranteed and work may be lost. If queue-master alone is disconnected, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}` rises while consumers fall, and queued shipment tasks remain unprocessed
- **Root Cause**: DNS failure, a blocked TCP 5672 path, packet loss, or latency interrupts one of two distinct AMQP connections: shipping publishes a task to RabbitMQ's `shipping-task` queue, and queue-master consumes that queue. The documented queue is non-durable, and the current shipping code catches publish exceptions and still returns the shipment, so the runbook must not assume publisher confirms, durable storage, or automatic replay
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_unacknowledged{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_consumers{queue="shipping-task"}`, and `rabbitmq_connections`; distinguish a publisher-side outage with no queue growth from a consumer-side outage with ready messages accumulating
2. Review `kubectl logs deployment/shipping -n <namespace> --since=30m` for `Unable to add to queue` or AMQP connection errors and `kubectl logs deployment/queue-master -n <namespace> --since=30m` for consumer connection or delivery failures; correlate the timestamps with RabbitMQ logs and the `shipping-rabbitmq` and `queue-master-rabbitmq` health results
3. [real/rcaeval_resource_faults.md] (score=0.21) # Resource Faults

## RES-MEM-001: Memory Leak Leading to Heap Exhaustion and OOMKilled
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-ticketinfo-service, ts-food-map-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: `container_memory_usage_bytes` climbs monotonically over minutes/hours without stabilizing; JVM `jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap"}` approaches `jvm_memory_max_bytes`; full GC frequency and duration increase (`jvm_gc_pause_seconds_sum`); eventually the pod is terminated with `OOMKilled` (ExitCode 137) visible in `kubectl describe pod`; `kube_pod_container_status_last_terminated_reason{reason="OOMKilled"}` equals 1; dependent services see connection-reset errors during the kill window
- **Root Cause**: A fault-injected unbounded in-memory cache, leaked collection, or un-closed stream accumulates objects faster than garbage collection can reclaim them. The JVM heap fills to its `-Xmx` limit, triggering continuous full-GC cycles that consume CPU and stall request processing. When the container's RSS exceeds the Kubernetes memory limit, the Linux OOM killer terminates the process (SIGKILL, exit code 137). If no readiness probe catches the degraded state, traffic continues to route to the pod during the GC death spiral
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `container_memory_usage_bytes{pod=~"<service>.*"}` over 1h for a monotonic upward trend; check `jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap"}` and `jvm_gc_pause_seconds_count{gc="G1 Old Generation"}` for full-GC spikes; alert on `container_memory_usage_bytes / container_spec_memory_limit_bytes > 0.85`
2. Search pod logs for `java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space` or `GC overhead limit exceeded`; inspect Jaeger traces for request latency spikes that correlate with GC pauses; review application logs for cache-size or queue-depth warnings
3. `kubectl describe pod <pod> | grep -A5 "Last State"` to confirm `OOMKilled` and exit code 137; `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- jcmd 1 GC.heap_info` for live heap information; `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- jmap -histo:live 1 | head -30` to identify dominant object types; `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- sh -c 'cat /sys/fs/cgroup/memory.peak 2>/dev/null || cat /sys/fs/cgroup/memory/memory.max_usage_in_bytes'` to inspect cgroup v2 or v1 peak usage
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the leaking pod from traffic, capture a heap histogram or dump if capacity permits, restart it, and scale known-good replicas to restore capacity; verify that memory requests and limits leave saf...

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f3-gc-restart-cycle.md] (score=0.20) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F3-02: JVM Memory Misconfiguration Causes GC Thrash and Restart Cycle
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-station-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Travel and station APIs show sawtooth latency, frequent full garbage collections, failed readiness probes, and periodic restarts; `rate(jvm_gc_pause_seconds_sum[5m])` and `jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap"}` rise together; upstream services observe HTTP 502 or 503 responses even before an OOM kill occurs
- **Root Cause**: The F3 configuration leaves too little usable heap for the live data set or reserves nearly all container memory for heap. The JVM spends increasing time in full GC, starves health-check and request threads, and is eventually restarted by a failed probe or killed when native overhead exceeds the cgroup limit
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `rate(jvm_gc_pause_seconds_sum{service=~"ts-travel-service|ts-station-service"}[5m])`, old-generation utilization, readiness, and restart counters; compare total GC pause time with request latency and memory limit headroom
2. Inspect GC logs for repeated full collections with little reclaimed memory and correlate pause windows with readiness failures and trace latency; check whether the problem began with a JVM flag or resource-limit change
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <pod> --previous` and `kubectl describe pod -n <namespace> <pod>` to identify probe failure versus OOM termination; inspect the running JVM arguments and pod limit with `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <pod> -- sh -c 'tr "\000" " " </proc/1/cmdline; echo'` and `kubectl get pod -n <namespace> <pod> -o jsonpath='{.spec.containers[0].resources.limits.memory}{"\n"}'`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — restore the last known-good heap and container settings, relax only an incorrectly aggressive probe while preserving failure detection, and roll pods gradually to avoid simultaneous capacity loss. Long-term — derive heap and non-heap budgets from load tests, set startup and readiness probes around observed GC behavior, alert on GC time ratio plus memory headroom, and block deployments whose JVM flags conflict with pod limits

## SS-RES-MEM-002: Carts Service Memory Leak Under Cart Churn
- **Affected Services**: carts
- **Fault Type**: Resource

- **Root Cause**: A defect in carts retains cart objects, request buffers, or other per-request state in an unbounded in-process collection, or fails to release resources on update/delete paths. 1. 2. 3. # Online Boutique Network Faults  
## OB-NET-LOSS-001: Packet Loss Between Frontend and Cart Service
- **Affected Services**: frontend, cartservice, redis-cart
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: Add-to-cart and view-cart requests fail intermittently while catalog browsing continues to work; frontend returns HTTP 500/503 or reports gRPC `UNAVAILABLE` and `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`; `rate(node_netstat_Tcp_RetransSegs[5m]) / rate(node_netstat_Tcp_OutSegs[5m])` rises on the relevant nodes; cart latency becomes bimodal; traces show frontend cart client spans with long gaps or errors and, for fully lost connections, no matching cartservice server span
- **Root Cause**: Packet loss on the frontend-to-cartservice gRPC path drops TCP segments or connection handshakes. - **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. 2. Compare successful and failed frontend → cartservice traces; missing cartservice server spans indicate handshake or pre-delivery loss, whereas long client spans with short server spans indicate retransmission delay on the path; correlate trace IDs with frontend connection-reset and cartservice cancellation logs
3. Production frontend and cart images may not include `tc`, `ss`, or `ping`; attach an authorized ephemeral container with `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<frontend-pod> -it --image=<approved-network-debug-image> --target=server --profile=general`, then run `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0`, `ss -ti dst <cart-pod-ip>`, and, when ICMP is permitted, `ping -c 50 <cart-pod-ip>` from the shared frontend network namespace

- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the verified loss injection, route away from impaired endp...

[synthesized/train-ticket-network-f7-external-payment-timeout.md] (score=0.52) # Train Ticket Network Faults

# Train Ticket Network Faults  
## TT-NET-F7-01: External Payment Gateway Timeout
- **Affected Services**: ts-inside-payment-service, ts-payment-service
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: Payments that require the external gateway remain pending or fail with HTTP 504, `SocketTimeoutException`, or `PaymentTimeoutException`; `payment_external_timeout_total` rises while internal-balance payments continue to succeed; traces end at the outbound payment span with no timely response
- **Root Cause**: The F7 fallback call from `ts-inside-payment-service` to the external payment endpoint exceeds its connect or response deadline because of network delay, packet loss, gateway overload, or an unreachable upstream. Unbounded or unsafe retries can extend user latency and risk duplicate charges
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `rate(payment_external_timeout_total[5m])`, `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le) (rate(http_client_request_duration_seconds_bucket{service="ts-inside-payment-service",target="external-payment"}[5m])))`, and outbound connection-error counts
2. Inspect payment traces for the external span's connect, TLS, and response phases; correlate the payment ID with timeout logs and verify whether multiple attempts reused the same idempotency key
3. From an authorized ephemeral network-debug container sharing the `ts-inside-payment-service` pod network namespace, run `kubectl debug -n <namespace> -it <inside-payment-pod> --target=<inside-payment-container> --image=<approved-network-debug-image> -- curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{time_connect} %{time_total}\n' --max-time <seconds> <external-payment-health-url>`; check DNS and egress policy without submitting a real charge
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — open the circuit breaker, place affected payments in a durable pending state, and permit only bounded retries with the original idempotency key; fail over only to an approved gateway. Long-term — use asynchronous payment confirmation and reconciliation, define connect and response budgets, monitor gateway SLOs, test loss and delay behavior, and enforce duplicate-charge protection

# Sock Shop Code-Level Faults  
## SS-CODE-RET-001: Payment Service Returns an Incorrect Authorization Result
- **Affected Services**: payment, orders
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: `POST /paymentAuth` returns HTTP 200 with an `authorised` Boolean that disagrees with the submitted amount and configured decline threshold; orders may continue when an over-threshold amount should be declined or reject an eligible amount. Transport metrics can remain normal because the response is syntactically valid
- **Root Cause**: A defect in payment's amount-threshold comparison or response mapping returns the wrong `authorised` value or message. The Sock Shop payment service makes its decision from `amount` only; it does not validate the supplied card, persist a payment transaction, or return a transaction ID
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `sum by (status_code) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{method="POST",path="/paymentAuth"}[5m]))`; if custom instrumentation exports `sockshop_payment_authorization_result_total`, inspect it; otherwise derive the authorization distribution from the service's structured `method=Authorise result=<bool>` logs
2. Inspect a redacted trace for the request amount, payment response, and subsequent orders state transition; compare the amount with the payment container's configured `-decline` argument using `kubectl get deployment/payment -n <namespace> -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[?(@.name=="payment")].args}'`, and review `kubectl logs deployment/payment -n <namespace> --since=30m | grep 'method=Authorise'`
3. Review `kubectl rollout history deployment/payment -n <namespace>` and the running image; from an authorized ephemeral test pod in staging, send boundary amounts immediately below, equal to, and above the configured threshold to `/paymentAuth` and compare the Boolean and message with the expected decision table
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — stop or roll back the faulty payment revision, prevent orders with ambiguous authorization results from advancing to shipping, and review affected orders by deployment window, amount, and recorded Boolean result; do not claim capture, refund, or transaction-ID reconciliation because the demo payment service has no such mechanism. Long-term — keep the authorization result typed and explicit, replace floating-point money with validated minor-unit arithmetic if the service is extended, add threshold boundary and contract t...

# Online Boutique Network Faults  
## OB-NET-LOSS-002: Packet Loss in the Checkout Payment-Shipping-Email Dependency Chain
- **Affected Services**: checkoutservice, paymentservice, shippingservice, emailservice
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: Checkout completes inconsistently or fails with HTTP 500/503/504; checkoutservice reports gRPC `UNAVAILABLE`, `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, `connection reset by peer`, or `broken pipe` for paymentservice, shippingservice, or emailservice; TCP retransmission ratio rises; traces contain a failed or unusually long dependency span, and retries may produce duplicate payment attempts or repeated confirmation email unless protected
- **Root Cause**: Packet loss on one or more checkoutservice dependency paths drops TCP segments during the checkout workflow. TCP retransmissions consume per-call deadlines, and a lost response can leave checkoutservice uncertain whether payment or shipping work completed, creating partial-completion risk if it retries a non-idempotent operation
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. In Prometheus, query `rate(node_netstat_Tcp_RetransSegs[5m]) / rate(node_netstat_Tcp_OutSegs[5m])` on checkout and dependency nodes, then break down `rate(grpc_client_handled_total{job="checkoutservice",grpc_service=~".*(Payment|Shipping|Email).*",grpc_code=~"UNAVAILABLE|DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"}[5m])` by `grpc_service` to identify the impaired edge
2. Inspect failed checkout traces in call order and compare client and server spans: a client error with no server span suggests pre-delivery loss, while a successful payment server span followed by a lost client response indicates an unknown-outcome transaction that must be reconciled before retrying; correlate the trace ID with checkoutservice and dependency logs for `UNAVAILABLE`, `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, `connection reset`, or duplicate-operation warnings
3. Because the checkoutservice image may not contain a shell or network tools, attach an authorized ephemeral container with `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<checkout-pod> -it --image=<approved-network-debug-image> --target=server --profile=general`; from the shared pod network namespace, run `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0`, `ss -ti`, `grpc_health_probe -addr=paymentservice:50051`, `grpc_health_probe -addr=shippingservice:50051`, and `grpc_health_probe -addr=emailservice:5000`

# Online Boutique Code-Level Faults  
## OB-CODE-PARAM-001: Checkout Sends Incorrect Payment Parameters
- **Affected Services**: checkoutservice, paymentservice, currencyservice
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: The amount or currency in checkoutservice's `ChargeRequest` differs from the validated order total. With a valid card, the stock mock paymentservice can still return a transaction ID because it validates card data but does not reconcile the amount with the cart; malformed or invalid card fields instead produce gRPC `INVALID_ARGUMENT`. If custom instrumentation exports `checkout_charge_amount_mismatch_total`, inspect it; otherwise derive the mismatch from redacted structured logs or trace attributes that contain only `Money` fields
- **Root Cause**: checkoutservice maps a stale total, wrong currency code, or incorrectly converted `Money` value into `ChargeRequest` after currency conversion. The protobuf represents money as `currency_code`, `units`, and `nanos`, but the stock paymentservice does not verify that the requested amount equals an independently known order total, so semantically wrong amounts can be accepted rather than rejected
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. If custom instrumentation exports `checkout_charge_amount_mismatch_total`, inspect it; otherwise compare redacted checkout calculation events with payment charge events by trace ID and transaction ID. Separately inspect gRPC status counts for `INVALID_ARGUMENT` only as evidence of card/request validation failures, not proof of an amount mismatch, and correlate changes with `kubectl rollout history deployment/checkoutservice -n <namespace>`
2. Open an affected trace and compare the cart subtotal, shipping cost, currency conversion outputs, final order total, and `ChargeRequest.amount` (`currency_code`, `units`, `nanos` only); review `kubectl logs deployment/checkoutservice -n <namespace> --since=30m` and paymentservice logs without collecting card numbers, CVV values, or other payment credentials
3. In a non-production environment, port-forward paymentservice and use a workstation-installed `grpcurl` with the repository's matching protobuf definition and approved synthetic card fixtures to compare a correct and defective request; inspect deployed images with `kubectl get deployment checkoutservice paymentservice -n <namespace> -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"="}{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].image}{"\n"}{end}'` rather than ...

# Sock Shop Code-Level Faults  
## SS-CODE-FUNC-001: Order Processing Skips Payment Authorization
- **Affected Services**: orders, payment, shipping
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: The orders service creates an order or requests shipping without first calling payment at `POST /paymentAuth`; order-create traffic remains present while payment-request traffic falls. Affected traces contain carts/user lookups and may contain an orders-to-shipping span, but no orders-to-payment span or `authorised=true` response
- **Root Cause**: A defective orders revision bypasses the payment invocation or the guard that rejects a false `authorised` result. The documented workflow is payment authorization, then the shipping request, then order persistence; skipping either payment step permits an invalid state transition
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{service="orders",method="POST",path="/orders",status_code="201"}[5m]))` with `sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{method="POST",path="/paymentAuth"}[5m]))`; if custom instrumentation exports `sockshop_payment_authorization_result_total`, inspect it; otherwise derive the authorization outcome from structured payment logs
2. Inspect affected traces for the required orders-to-payment HTTP span before orders-to-shipping and database persistence; correlate trace IDs and timestamps in `kubectl logs deployment/orders -n <namespace> --since=1h` and `kubectl logs deployment/payment -n <namespace> --since=1h` without printing request bodies, card details, cookies, or authorization headers
3. Review `kubectl rollout history deployment/orders -n <namespace>` and the running image; in staging, submit an order below the configured payment decline threshold and verify exactly one `/paymentAuth` call and an `authorised=true` response occur before the shipping call and order save
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — stop or roll back the defective orders revision, pause fulfillment for orders created by that revision without corresponding payment-call evidence, restore the payment call and authorization guard, and review each affected demo order before resuming shipping. Long-term — enforce the payment-before-shipping-before-persistence sequence with an explicit state machine and integration tests; if a real payment provider replaces the mock, add durable payment references and idempotency keys rather than assuming the current service supp...

# Online Boutique Network Faults  
## OB-NET-DELAY-001: Catalog Path Latency Delays Product and Recommendation Responses
- **Affected Services**: frontend, productcatalogservice, recommendationservice
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: The home page and product-detail pages load slowly, recommendation panels time out, and frontend may return HTTP 504; p99 gRPC client latency rises for frontend → productcatalogservice and recommendationservice → productcatalogservice; traces show a nearly constant added delay on catalog calls, with `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` once the caller budget is exceeded, while CPU and memory on all three services remain near baseline
- **Root Cause**: A `tc netem` rule, service-mesh fault rule, or degraded network path adds latency to traffic reaching productcatalogservice. Both frontend and recommendationservice depend on its gRPC API, so the delay affects direct catalog rendering and the nested recommendation lookup, lengthening the critical path and causing caller queues to build
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. In Prometheus, compare `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le, grpc_service) (rate(grpc_client_handling_seconds_bucket{job=~"frontend|recommendationservice",grpc_service=~".*ProductCatalog.*"}[5m])))` before and during the incident; check `rate(grpc_client_handled_total{job=~"frontend|recommendationservice",grpc_code="DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"}[5m])` and confirm local CPU/memory are not saturated
2. Inspect Jaeger traces for frontend → productcatalogservice and frontend → recommendationservice → productcatalogservice; a similar additive gap before catalog responses, without elevated catalog application self-time, indicates path delay rather than slow catalog code
3. Use authorized ephemeral containers because the application images may lack a shell and diagnostic binaries: `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<frontend-pod> -it --image=<approved-network-debug-image> --target=server --profile=general` and the equivalent command for `<recommendation-pod>`; from each shared network namespace, time `grpc_health_probe -addr=productcatalogservice:3550`, then inspect the catalog pod's qdisc with the same method and `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0`
4. Review service-mesh configuration and endpoint placement with `kubectl get virtualservice,destinationrule -n <namespace> -o yaml` and `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=productcatalogservice -o wide`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the verified delay ...

# Sock Shop Resource Faults  
## SS-RES-CPU-002: Catalogue Service CPU Saturation
- **Affected Services**: catalogue
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Go catalogue pods consume sustained CPU near their limit; product-list and search latency rises; front-end pages become slow or return HTTP 502/504; CFS throttling increases; traces show long catalogue processing time even when catalogue-db query time remains normal
- **Root Cause**: An injected or regressed CPU-bound path in catalogue—such as inefficient filtering, repeated serialization, or unbounded result processing—consumes the container's CPU. Cgroup throttling then delays request goroutines and MySQL response handling, so the database can appear implicated even when its own latency is normal. A specific regular-expression or cardinality mechanism must be proven from the deployed code and profile rather than assumed
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m]))` and `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m])) / sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_periods_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m]))`; compare them with `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{pod=~"catalogue-.*"}[5m])))`. If custom instrumentation exports `mysql_global_status_threads_running`, inspect it; otherwise derive database pressure from catalogue/database structured logs and database-native telemetry
2. Inspect traces for catalogue spans with high service self-time; if the tracing setup emits database child spans, compare their duration, otherwise correlate catalogue request timing with catalogue-db slow-query evidence. Review `kubectl logs deployment/catalogue -n <namespace> --since=30m` for slow requests and compare controlled request shapes or result sizes without recording sensitive inputs
3. Run `kubectl top pods -n <namespace> -l name=catalogue --containers` and `kubectl rollout history deployment/catalogue -n <namespace>`. The upstream catalogue binary does not enable a pprof endpoint by default; profile a reproduction in staging with an explicitly instrumented build, or use a separately approved and protected production profiling mechanism rather than assuming `/debug/pprof` exists
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — route traffic away from hot pods, rate-limit or disable ...

# Sock Shop Resource Faults  
## SS-RES-MEM-002: Carts Service Memory Leak Under Cart Churn
- **Affected Services**: carts
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Java carts pod memory grows as carts are created and updated, does not return to its earlier post-GC baseline after load falls, and approaches the container limit; garbage-collection time and HTTP latency increase; the container may be terminated with `OOMKilled`; front-end cart operations may return HTTP 500/503 while carts-db remains reachable
- **Root Cause**: A defect in carts retains cart objects, request buffers, or other per-request state in an unbounded in-process collection, or fails to release resources on update/delete paths. The retained objects survive garbage collection until the JVM heap or container memory limit is exhausted. This diagnosis requires heap evidence; persistent MongoDB documents alone do not constitute a JVM memory leak
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `container_memory_working_set_bytes{pod=~"carts-.*",container="carts"}`, `jvm_memory_bytes_used{pod=~"carts-.*",area="heap"}`, and `rate(jvm_gc_collection_seconds_sum{pod=~"carts-.*"}[5m])` with `sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{pod=~"carts-.*"}[5m]))`; a rising post-GC heap baseline after request volume subsides supports retained-memory suspicion
2. Correlate the growth window with traces for cart add/update/delete paths and inspect current and previous logs using `kubectl logs deployment/carts -n <namespace> --since=1h` and `kubectl logs <carts-pod> -n <namespace> --previous` for `OutOfMemoryError`, GC overhead, or MongoDB resource errors; do not infer the retained type from logs alone
3. Confirm the termination reason with `kubectl get pod <carts-pod> -n <namespace> -o jsonpath='{.status.containerStatuses[?(@.name=="carts")].lastState.terminated.reason}'` and check live usage with `kubectl top pod <carts-pod> -n <namespace> --containers`; because the carts image is JRE-based, do not assume `jcmd` exists in it—when authorized, attach a compatible JDK ephemeral debug container with `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<carts-pod> -it --image=eclipse-temurin:8-jdk --target=carts`, identify the actual carts Java PID in the shared process namespace, and run `jcmd <java-pid> GC.class_histogram`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — preserve safe heap evidence when feasible, drain and restart the leaking carts pod, roll back the correlated change, and keep traffic on known-go...

# Sock Shop Code-Level Faults  
## SS-CODE-FUNC-002: Completed Order Fails to Clear the Customer Cart
- **Affected Services**: front-end, orders, carts
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: The order request returns HTTP 201 and the order is stored, but the same items remain in the customer's cart; a repeated checkout can create another order. The expected follow-up `DELETE /cart` request from the browser to front-end, or the downstream `DELETE /carts/{customerId}` request from front-end to carts, is absent or fails after order creation
- **Root Cause**: In the documented Sock Shop flow, browser-side front-end code calls `deleteCart()` only after a successful order response; front-end then proxies the delete to carts. A missing callback, premature navigation, JavaScript error, or ignored delete failure leaves the cart intact. The orders service does not own or invoke the carts cleanup operation
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. If custom instrumentation exports `sockshop_checkout_success_total` and `sockshop_cart_delete_total`, inspect them; otherwise derive the signal from structured logs by comparing HTTP 201 order responses with subsequent successful cart deletes for the same authenticated customer and release window
2. Inspect an affected browser or end-to-end trace for `POST /orders`, followed by `DELETE /cart` and downstream `DELETE /carts/{customerId}`; correlate redacted timestamps and trace IDs in `kubectl logs deployment/front-end -n <namespace> --since=1h` and `kubectl logs deployment/carts -n <namespace> --since=1h` without copying cookies, card data, or authorization headers
3. Review `kubectl rollout history deployment/front-end -n <namespace>` and the deployed `order()`/`deleteCart()` code path; in staging, place one uniquely tagged order, wait for the asynchronous delete, and confirm through the carts API that the customer cart no longer exists before attempting a second checkout
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — roll back or patch front-end so a successful order reliably triggers and verifies cart deletion, temporarily block repeated checkout for affected sessions, and clear only carts tied to verified completed orders through an audited procedure. Long-term — move post-order cleanup into a retryable server-side workflow or make the existing front-end request observable and failure-aware, make order creation idempotent, add end-to-end assertions for post-checkout cart state, and alert on successful-o...

[synthesized/05_sock-shop_network_rabbitmq-amqp-failure.md] (score=0.53) # Sock Shop Network Faults

# Sock Shop Network Faults  
## SS-NET-AMQP-001: RabbitMQ Connectivity Failure Disrupts Shipping Tasks
- **Affected Services**: shipping, queue-master, rabbitmq
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: If shipping cannot publish, its HTTP endpoint can still accept the shipment while logging that it could not add the task to the queue, so no RabbitMQ backlog is guaranteed and work may be lost. If queue-master alone is disconnected, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}` rises while consumers fall, and queued shipment tasks remain unprocessed
- **Root Cause**: DNS failure, a blocked TCP 5672 path, packet loss, or latency interrupts one of two distinct AMQP connections: shipping publishes a task to RabbitMQ's `shipping-task` queue, and queue-master consumes that queue. The documented queue is non-durable, and the current shipping code catches publish exceptions and still returns the shipment, so the runbook must not assume publisher confirms, durable storage, or automatic replay
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_unacknowledged{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_consumers{queue="shipping-task"}`, and `rabbitmq_connections`; distinguish a publisher-side outage with no queue growth from a consumer-side outage with ready messages accumulating
2. Review `kubectl logs deployment/shipping -n <namespace> --since=30m` for `Unable to add to queue` or AMQP connection errors and `kubectl logs deployment/queue-master -n <namespace> --since=30m` for consumer connection or delivery failures; correlate the timestamps with RabbitMQ logs and the `shipping-rabbitmq` and `queue-master-rabbitmq` health results
3. Verify `kubectl get service rabbitmq -n <namespace>` and `kubectl get endpointslice -n <namespace> -l kubernetes.io/service-name=rabbitmq`; from an authorized ephemeral debug container attached to the affected application pod, test DNS and TCP connectivity to `rabbitmq:5672`, then inspect only the NetworkPolicies and fault-injection resources governing that path
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — restore RabbitMQ endpoints and TCP 5672 reachability, remove confirmed fault-injection rules, and restart only connections that do not recover. If queue-master alone failed, let it drain the existing `shipping-task` backlog at a controlled rate; if shipping failed to publish, reconstruct missing tasks from authoritative completed-order record...

[synthesized/03_sock-shop_resource_catalogue-cpu-hog.md] (score=0.53) # Sock Shop Resource Faults

# Sock Shop Resource Faults  
## SS-RES-CPU-002: Catalogue Service CPU Saturation
- **Affected Services**: catalogue
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Go catalogue pods consume sustained CPU near their limit; product-list and search latency rises; front-end pages become slow or return HTTP 502/504; CFS throttling increases; traces show long catalogue processing time even when catalogue-db query time remains normal
- **Root Cause**: An injected or regressed CPU-bound path in catalogue—such as inefficient filtering, repeated serialization, or unbounded result processing—consumes the container's CPU. Cgroup throttling then delays request goroutines and MySQL response handling, so the database can appear implicated even when its own latency is normal. A specific regular-expression or cardinality mechanism must be proven from the deployed code and profile rather than assumed
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m]))` and `sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m])) / sum by (pod) (rate(container_cpu_cfs_periods_total{pod=~"catalogue-.*",container="catalogue"}[5m]))`; compare them with `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{pod=~"catalogue-.*"}[5m])))`. If custom instrumentation exports `mysql_global_status_threads_running`, inspect it; otherwise derive database pressure from catalogue/database structured logs and database-native telemetry
2. Inspect traces for catalogue spans with high service self-time; if the tracing setup emits database child spans, compare their duration, otherwise correlate catalogue request timing with catalogue-db slow-query evidence. Review `kubectl logs deployment/catalogue -n <namespace> --since=30m` for slow requests and compare controlled request shapes or result sizes without recording sensitive inputs
3. Run `kubectl top pods -n <namespace> -l name=catalogue --containers` and `kubectl rollout history deployment/catalogue -n <namespace>`. The upstream catalogue binary does not enable a pprof endpoint by default; profile a reproduction in staging with an explicitly instrumented build, or use a separately approved and protected production profiling mechanism rather than assuming `/debug/pprof` exists
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — route traffic away from hot pods, rate-limit or disable ...

[synthesized/train-ticket-code-f10-wrong-contacts-api.md] (score=0.38) # Train Ticket Code-Level Faults

# Train Ticket Code-Level Faults  
## TT-CODE-F10-01: Reservation Flow Calls the Wrong Contacts API
- **Affected Services**: ts-preserve-service, ts-contacts-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: A specific contact type or reservation path fails with HTTP 400, 404, 405, or 500 while ordinary reservations succeed; `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}` is concentrated in one request subtype; traces show an unexpected contacts endpoint or response schema
- **Root Cause**: The F10 branch-selection logic in `ts-preserve-service` maps an edge-case reservation to the wrong `ts-contacts-service` operation. The called endpoint expects different parameters or semantics, so its response cannot be used by the reservation workflow
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Break down preserve failures by contact type and booking path; if custom instrumentation exports `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}`, graph `rate(reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}[5m])`, otherwise derive the cohort from structured preserve-service error logs and compare it with total preserve requests
2. Diff successful and failed traces at the contacts call, including route template, method, sanitized request shape, response status, and deployed versions; correlate the failing branch with preserve-service logs
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-preserve-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` and `kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/ts-contacts-service --since=15m | grep '<trace-id>'` to verify the selected endpoint and rejection reason
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — route the affected scenario to the correct contacts operation or disable only that path while preserving standard reservations; roll back if the mapping changed in the current release. Long-term — use a typed client with one explicit discriminator, validate inputs before dispatch, add consumer-driven contract tests for every branch, and include the edge case in end-to-end reservation tests

[real/rcaeval_code_faults.md] (score=0.35) # Code-Level Faults

# Code-Level Faults  
## CODE-PARAM-001: Incorrect Parameters Passed in Inter-Service RPC/HTTP Calls
- **Affected Services**: ts-preserve-service, ts-security-service, ts-travel-plan-service, ts-route-service, ts-order-service, ts-station-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Downstream service returns HTTP 400 Bad Request, 422 Unprocessable Entity, or a business-logic error with an error message indicating type mismatch or schema violation; Prometheus `http_requests_total{status="400",service="<downstream>"}` increases; callers receive unexpected error responses that propagate as HTTP 500 to the end user; Jaeger traces show the downstream span completing quickly with an error tag; partial data corruption if the downstream silently accepts the wrong-typed parameter (e.g., truncating a string-to-int cast)
- **Root Cause**: A fault-injected or buggy code path sends a request to a downstream service with an incorrect data type (e.g., `String` where `Integer` is expected), wrong field mapping (e.g., `orderId` sent as `trainNumber`), or a schema-incompatible payload (e.g., date in `MM/DD/YYYY` instead of `ISO 8601`). The downstream service's deserialization or validation layer rejects the malformed input, or worse, silently coerces it to a wrong value that corrupts downstream processing
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Check `http_requests_total{status="400",path=~"<downstream-endpoint>"}` and `http_requests_total{status="422"}` in Prometheus for the affected downstream service; correlate the spike with the caller service's `http_client_requests_total{status="400"}`
2. Inspect the downstream service's logs for validation error messages (e.g., `JSON parse error: Cannot deserialize value of type 'Integer' from String`, `MethodArgumentTypeMismatchException`, `ConstraintViolationException`); in Jaeger traces, compare the request payload in the caller's outbound span with the downstream's inbound span metadata
3. `kubectl logs -l app=<downstream-service> --tail=300 | grep -iE "400|Bad Request|parse error|type mismatch|deserializ"` to identify the rejected field; `kubectl exec -it <caller-pod> -- curl -v -X POST -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '<reproduced-payload>' http://<downstream>:8080/<endpoint>` to reproduce the exact failure; diff the caller's request DTO against the downstream's expected DTO schema
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — fix the parameter type or field mapping in the caller service's request constructi...
runbook_retrieval{"k": 5, "query": "ts-admin-route-service"}
[real/logic-faults.md] (score=0.33) # Logic Faults

## F10: Wrong API Invocation in Specific Reservation Scenario
- **Affected Services**: Contacts-Service, Reservation-Service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Ticket reservation fails in a specific edge case (e.g., a particular contact type or booking path); HTTP 500 or business-logic error returned; Prometheus `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}` increments for a subset of reservation attempts
- **Root Cause**: Contacts-Service exposes two separate APIs for the reservation flow, each handling a different business scenario. The routing logic in Reservation-Service does not correctly distinguish between the two scenarios, invoking the wrong Contacts API for a specific edge case. This produces an unexpected response that the reservation flow cannot process
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Check `reservation_failures_total{reason="contact_api_error"}` in Prometheus; filter by request attributes (contact type, booking path) to identify the failing subset
2. Inspect Jaeger traces for failed reservations: compare the Contacts-Service API endpoint called in successful vs. failed traces; search Reservation-Service logs for the request payload that triggers the wrong API branch
3. `kubectl logs -l app=reservation-service --tail=500 | grep -E "contacts.*api\|api.*contacts"` and `kubectl logs -l app=contacts-service --tail=500 | grep "404\|405\|unexpected"` to identify the misrouted API call
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — fix the conditional logic in Reservation-Service that selects the Contacts API endpoint; add explicit validation of the scenario type before dispatching. Long-term — consolidate the two Contacts APIs into a single endpoint with a discriminator parameter; add contract tests between Reservation-Service and Contacts-Service to catch routing regressions

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.32) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together

## CODE-PARAM-002: Missing Required Parameters in Inter-Service Requests
- **Affected Services**: ts-order-service, ts-contacts-service, ts-train-service, ts-config-service
- **Fault Type**: Code-level
- **Symptom**: Downstream service returns HTTP 400 Bad Request with a message indicating a required field is null or absent (e.g., `"field 'accountId' must not be null"`); Prometheus `http_requests_total{status="400",service="<downstream>"}` increases; if the downstream lacks proper validation, it may throw a `NullPointerException` resulting in HTTP 500; end users see a generic error or empty results for the affected operation
- **Root Cause**: A fault-injected or buggy code path omits a required field from the outgoing request payload or query parameters. The missing field may be due to a null variable that was never populated (e.g., a preceding service call failed silently and returned null), a conditional code branch that skips field assignment, or a DTO mapping that drops the field during serialization. The downstream service either rejects the request at its validation layer (clean failure) or proceeds with a null value, triggering a NullPointerException deeper in the processing logic
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Check `http_requests_total{status="400"}` and `http_requests_total{status="500"}` on the downstream service in Prometheus; filter by endpoint path to isolate the affected operation; check caller-side `http_client_requests_total{status="400"}` for the same time window
2. Search downstream service logs for `MethodArgumentNotValidException`, `MissingServletRequestParameterException`, `NullPointerException`, or `Required field .* is missing` messages; inspect Jaeger traces for the failing request — check the caller's outbound span attributes for null or absent fields; look for a preceding span in the caller that returns null (the source of the missing value)
3. `kubectl logs -l app=<downstream> --tail=300 | grep -iE "required|missing|null|NotValid|400"` to identify which field is missing; `kubectl exec -it <caller-pod> -- curl -v -X POST -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{}' http://<downstream>:8080/<endpoint>` to test with an empty payload and read the validation error response; compare the request body against the `@NotNull` / `@Valid` annotations in the downstream's DTO class
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — fix the caller code to populate the required field; if the field depends on an upstream call, add a nul...

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-01: Basic Info Thread Pool Exhaustion
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-travel-service, ts-ticketinfo-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Ticket-information requests slow down or return HTTP 504 errors during burst traffic; `jvm_threads_live_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` and the application worker-pool active count reach their configured maxima; executor queue depth rises while pod CPU can remain below its limit; traces show long gaps before work begins in `ts-basic-service`
- **Root Cause**: The F5 asynchronous ticket-information path submits blocking dependency calls to a bounded shared executor. Slow tasks occupy every worker, new tasks accumulate in the queue or are rejected, and callers time out before the service can schedule their work
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `executor_active_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` with `executor_pool_max_threads` and graph `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-basic-service"}` alongside `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-basic-service",status=~"5.."}[5m])`
2. Inspect a failed distributed trace from `ts-travel-service` through `ts-basic-service`; confirm that the server span has high scheduling or queue wait and correlate it with `RejectedExecutionException` or `TimeoutException` in the same trace ID
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump to container logs, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'RUNNABLE|WAITING|BLOCKED|RejectedExecutionException|TimeoutException'`; use an approved JDK diagnostic container only if `SIGQUIT` dumps are disabled
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — shed nonessential ticket-info traffic, open a circuit breaker for the slow dependency, and scale only after confirming downstream capacity; if a release changed executor behavior, roll it back. Long-term — isolate blocking dependencies in separate bounded pools, enforce per-call deadlines and queue limits, add back-pressure or fallback responses, size executors from measured concurrency, and load-test the F5 path
