[real/rcaeval_resource_faults.md] (score=0.41) # Resource Faults

# Resource Faults  
## RES-CPU-001: CPU Hogging via Infinite Loop or Runaway Computation
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-order-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Sustained CPU utilization at or near 100% on affected pod(s); response latency climbs sharply across dependent services; Prometheus `container_cpu_usage_seconds_total` rate pegs at the pod's CPU limit; `node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}` drops toward zero on the host; HTTP 504 Gateway Timeout or gRPC DEADLINE_EXCEEDED errors propagate upstream; Kubernetes liveness probes may start failing, triggering pod restarts (`kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total` increments)
- **Root Cause**: A fault-injected or buggy code path enters an infinite loop, unbounded recursive computation, or tight spin-wait without yielding. The thread (or all threads in a thread pool) saturate the CPU allocation, starving the event loop, health-check handlers, and garbage collection. In containerized environments the cgroup CPU quota is exhausted, causing kernel-level throttling (`container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total` rises) that makes the service appear frozen to callers
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `rate(container_cpu_usage_seconds_total{pod=~"<service>.*"}[2m])` and compare against `kube_pod_container_resource_limits{resource="cpu"}`; check `rate(container_cpu_cfs_throttled_periods_total[2m]) / rate(container_cpu_cfs_periods_total[2m])` for throttle ratio > 0.5; inspect `node_load1` on the host node
2. Search application logs for absence of normal request-processing entries during the spike window (log silence indicates the event loop is blocked); inspect Jaeger traces for spans that never complete or show multi-second durations on a single service; look for repeated GC pause log entries (`gc_pause_seconds_sum` spike)
3. `kubectl top pod -l app=<service> --containers` to confirm CPU consumption; `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- top -bn1 -H | head -20` to identify the hot thread; for JVM services: `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- jstack 1 | grep -A15 "RUNNABLE"` to capture the stack trace of spinning threads; `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- sh -c 'cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cpu.stat 2>/dev/null || cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cpu/cpu.stat'` to inspect cgroup v2 or v1 throttling counters
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the affected pod from traffic, roll back or disable the runaway code path, and restart the pod (`kubectl delete pod <pod>`) only...

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.47) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together
runbook_retrieval{"query": "ts-order-service high delay"}
[synthesized/06_online_boutique_catalog_recommendation_network_delay.md] (score=0.46) # Online Boutique Network Faults

# Online Boutique Network Faults  
## OB-NET-DELAY-001: Catalog Path Latency Delays Product and Recommendation Responses
- **Affected Services**: frontend, productcatalogservice, recommendationservice
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: The home page and product-detail pages load slowly, recommendation panels time out, and frontend may return HTTP 504; p99 gRPC client latency rises for frontend → productcatalogservice and recommendationservice → productcatalogservice; traces show a nearly constant added delay on catalog calls, with `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` once the caller budget is exceeded, while CPU and memory on all three services remain near baseline
- **Root Cause**: A `tc netem` rule, service-mesh fault rule, or degraded network path adds latency to traffic reaching productcatalogservice. Both frontend and recommendationservice depend on its gRPC API, so the delay affects direct catalog rendering and the nested recommendation lookup, lengthening the critical path and causing caller queues to build
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. In Prometheus, compare `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le, grpc_service) (rate(grpc_client_handling_seconds_bucket{job=~"frontend|recommendationservice",grpc_service=~".*ProductCatalog.*"}[5m])))` before and during the incident; check `rate(grpc_client_handled_total{job=~"frontend|recommendationservice",grpc_code="DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"}[5m])` and confirm local CPU/memory are not saturated
2. Inspect Jaeger traces for frontend → productcatalogservice and frontend → recommendationservice → productcatalogservice; a similar additive gap before catalog responses, without elevated catalog application self-time, indicates path delay rather than slow catalog code
3. Use authorized ephemeral containers because the application images may lack a shell and diagnostic binaries: `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<frontend-pod> -it --image=<approved-network-debug-image> --target=server --profile=general` and the equivalent command for `<recommendation-pod>`; from each shared network namespace, time `grpc_health_probe -addr=productcatalogservice:3550`, then inspect the catalog pod's qdisc with the same method and `tc -s qdisc show dev eth0`
4. Review service-mesh configuration and endpoint placement with `kubectl get virtualservice,destinationrule -n <namespace> -o yaml` and `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=productcatalogservice -o wide`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — remove the verified delay ...
runbook_retrieval{"query": "ts-order-service high delay"}
[synthesized/train-ticket-network-f1-cancellation-message-reordering.md] (score=0.46) # Train Ticket Network Faults

# Train Ticket Network Faults  
## TT-NET-F1-01: Cancellation Events Complete Out of Order Under Network Delay
- **Affected Services**: ts-cancel-service, ts-inside-payment-service, ts-order-service
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: Cancelled orders remain in `CANCELLING` or revert from `CANCEL` to an earlier state; `order_status_mismatch_total` rises during latency incidents; traces show the refund path and final status-reset call completing in a different order from the cancellation workflow's intended sequence
- **Root Cause**: The F1 workflow runs refund and order-status work asynchronously without enforcing completion order. Variable network delay lets the final `CANCEL` update complete before the delayed refund path finishes its earlier `CANCELLING` write, allowing the stale transition to overwrite the correct state
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `order_status_transitions_total{from="CANCELLING",to="CANCEL"}` with cancellation volume and graph HTTP client latency, timeout count, and retry count across `ts-cancel-service`, `ts-inside-payment-service`, and the applicable order service
2. Reconstruct one affected order from trace and log timestamps using `order_id`, trace ID, operation name, and completion time; verify that the delayed `CANCELLING` write was accepted after the final `CANCEL` transition
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> -l app=ts-cancel-service --since=15m | grep '<order-id>'` and repeat for `ts-inside-payment-service`, `ts-order-service`, and `ts-order-other-service`; correlate the entries with the matching trace spans and pod restart timestamps
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — pause or disable the faulty cancellation path, reconcile affected orders from the authoritative payment result, and reject stale state writes using a version check. Long-term — encode cancellation as an ordered saga or state machine, make each step idempotent, attach monotonic state versions, and test delay, duplication, timeout, and retry behavior explicitly
runbook_retrieval{"query": "ts-order-service high delay"}

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-travel-worker-saturation.md] (score=0.52) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f5-basic-info-thread-pool.md] (score=0.49) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-01: Basic Info Thread Pool Exhaustion
- **Affected Services**: ts-basic-service, ts-travel-service, ts-ticketinfo-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Ticket-information requests slow down or return HTTP 504 errors during burst traffic; `jvm_threads_live_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` and the application worker-pool active count reach their configured maxima; executor queue depth rises while pod CPU can remain below its limit; traces show long gaps before work begins in `ts-basic-service`
- **Root Cause**: The F5 asynchronous ticket-information path submits blocking dependency calls to a bounded shared executor. Slow tasks occupy every worker, new tasks accumulate in the queue or are rejected, and callers time out before the service can schedule their work
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `executor_active_threads{service="ts-basic-service"}` with `executor_pool_max_threads` and graph `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-basic-service"}` alongside `rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{service="ts-basic-service",status=~"5.."}[5m])`
2. Inspect a failed distributed trace from `ts-travel-service` through `ts-basic-service`; confirm that the server span has high scheduling or queue wait and correlate it with `RejectedExecutionException` or `TimeoutException` in the same trace ID
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump to container logs, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-basic-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'RUNNABLE|WAITING|BLOCKED|RejectedExecutionException|TimeoutException'`; use an approved JDK diagnostic container only if `SIGQUIT` dumps are disabled
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — shed nonessential ticket-info traffic, open a circuit breaker for the slow dependency, and scale only after confirming downstream capacity; if a release changed executor behavior, roll it back. Long-term — isolate blocking dependencies in separate bounded pools, enforce per-call deadlines and queue limits, add back-pressure or fallback responses, size executors from measured concurrency, and load-test the F5 path

[synthesized/train-ticket-resource-f3-gc-restart-cycle.md] (score=0.46) # Train Ticket Resource Faults

# Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F3-02: JVM Memory Misconfiguration Causes GC Thrash and Restart Cycle
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-station-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Travel and station APIs show sawtooth latency, frequent full garbage collections, failed readiness probes, and periodic restarts; `rate(jvm_gc_pause_seconds_sum[5m])` and `jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap"}` rise together; upstream services observe HTTP 502 or 503 responses even before an OOM kill occurs
- **Root Cause**: The F3 configuration leaves too little usable heap for the live data set or reserves nearly all container memory for heap. The JVM spends increasing time in full GC, starves health-check and request threads, and is eventually restarted by a failed probe or killed when native overhead exceeds the cgroup limit
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `rate(jvm_gc_pause_seconds_sum{service=~"ts-travel-service|ts-station-service"}[5m])`, old-generation utilization, readiness, and restart counters; compare total GC pause time with request latency and memory limit headroom
2. Inspect GC logs for repeated full collections with little reclaimed memory and correlate pause windows with readiness failures and trace latency; check whether the problem began with a JVM flag or resource-limit change
3. Run `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <pod> --previous` and `kubectl describe pod -n <namespace> <pod>` to identify probe failure versus OOM termination; inspect the running JVM arguments and pod limit with `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <pod> -- sh -c 'tr "\000" " " </proc/1/cmdline; echo'` and `kubectl get pod -n <namespace> <pod> -o jsonpath='{.spec.containers[0].resources.limits.memory}{"\n"}'`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — restore the last known-good heap and container settings, relax only an incorrectly aggressive probe while preserving failure detection, and roll pods gradually to avoid simultaneous capacity loss. Long-term — derive heap and non-heap budgets from load tests, set startup and readiness probes around observed GC behavior, alert on GC time ratio plus memory headroom, and block deployments whose JVM flags conflict with pod limits

# Train Ticket Network Faults  
## TT-NET-F7-01: External Payment Gateway Timeout
- **Affected Services**: ts-inside-payment-service, ts-payment-service
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: Payments that require the external gateway remain pending or fail with HTTP 504, `SocketTimeoutException`, or `PaymentTimeoutException`; `payment_external_timeout_total` rises while internal-balance payments continue to succeed; traces end at the outbound payment span with no timely response
- **Root Cause**: The F7 fallback call from `ts-inside-payment-service` to the external payment endpoint exceeds its connect or response deadline because of network delay, packet loss, gateway overload, or an unreachable upstream. Unbounded or unsafe retries can extend user latency and risk duplicate charges
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `rate(payment_external_timeout_total[5m])`, `histogram_quantile(0.99, sum by (le) (rate(http_client_request_duration_seconds_bucket{service="ts-inside-payment-service",target="external-payment"}[5m])))`, and outbound connection-error counts
2. Inspect payment traces for the external span's connect, TLS, and response phases; correlate the payment ID with timeout logs and verify whether multiple attempts reused the same idempotency key
3. From an authorized ephemeral network-debug container sharing the `ts-inside-payment-service` pod network namespace, run `kubectl debug -n <namespace> -it <inside-payment-pod> --target=<inside-payment-container> --image=<approved-network-debug-image> -- curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{time_connect} %{time_total}\n' --max-time <seconds> <external-payment-health-url>`; check DNS and egress policy without submitting a real charge
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — open the circuit breaker, place affected payments in a durable pending state, and permit only bounded retries with the original idempotency key; fail over only to an approved gateway. Long-term — use asynchronous payment confirmation and reconciliation, define connect and response budgets, monitor gateway SLOs, test loss and delay behavior, and enforce duplicate-charge protection

[synthesized/04_sock-shop_resource_carts-memory-leak.md] (score=0.57) # Sock Shop Resource Faults

# Sock Shop Resource Faults  
## SS-RES-MEM-002: Carts Service Memory Leak Under Cart Churn
- **Affected Services**: carts
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Java carts pod memory grows as carts are created and updated, does not return to its earlier post-GC baseline after load falls, and approaches the container limit; garbage-collection time and HTTP latency increase; the container may be terminated with `OOMKilled`; front-end cart operations may return HTTP 500/503 while carts-db remains reachable
- **Root Cause**: A defect in carts retains cart objects, request buffers, or other per-request state in an unbounded in-process collection, or fails to release resources on update/delete paths. The retained objects survive garbage collection until the JVM heap or container memory limit is exhausted. This diagnosis requires heap evidence; persistent MongoDB documents alone do not constitute a JVM memory leak
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Compare `container_memory_working_set_bytes{pod=~"carts-.*",container="carts"}`, `jvm_memory_bytes_used{pod=~"carts-.*",area="heap"}`, and `rate(jvm_gc_collection_seconds_sum{pod=~"carts-.*"}[5m])` with `sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_count{pod=~"carts-.*"}[5m]))`; a rising post-GC heap baseline after request volume subsides supports retained-memory suspicion
2. Correlate the growth window with traces for cart add/update/delete paths and inspect current and previous logs using `kubectl logs deployment/carts -n <namespace> --since=1h` and `kubectl logs <carts-pod> -n <namespace> --previous` for `OutOfMemoryError`, GC overhead, or MongoDB resource errors; do not infer the retained type from logs alone
3. Confirm the termination reason with `kubectl get pod <carts-pod> -n <namespace> -o jsonpath='{.status.containerStatuses[?(@.name=="carts")].lastState.terminated.reason}'` and check live usage with `kubectl top pod <carts-pod> -n <namespace> --containers`; because the carts image is JRE-based, do not assume `jcmd` exists in it—when authorized, attach a compatible JDK ephemeral debug container with `kubectl debug -n <namespace> pod/<carts-pod> -it --image=eclipse-temurin:8-jdk --target=carts`, identify the actual carts Java PID in the shared process namespace, and run `jcmd <java-pid> GC.class_histogram`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — preserve safe heap evidence when feasible, drain and restart the leaking carts pod, roll back the correlated change, and keep traffic on known-go...

# Sock Shop Network Faults  
## SS-NET-AMQP-001: RabbitMQ Connectivity Failure Disrupts Shipping Tasks
- **Affected Services**: shipping, queue-master, rabbitmq
- **Fault Type**: Network
- **Symptom**: If shipping cannot publish, its HTTP endpoint can still accept the shipment while logging that it could not add the task to the queue, so no RabbitMQ backlog is guaranteed and work may be lost. If queue-master alone is disconnected, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}` rises while consumers fall, and queued shipment tasks remain unprocessed
- **Root Cause**: DNS failure, a blocked TCP 5672 path, packet loss, or latency interrupts one of two distinct AMQP connections: shipping publishes a task to RabbitMQ's `shipping-task` queue, and queue-master consumes that queue. The documented queue is non-durable, and the current shipping code catches publish exceptions and still returns the shipment, so the runbook must not assume publisher confirms, durable storage, or automatic replay
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Query `rabbitmq_queue_messages_ready{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_messages_unacknowledged{queue="shipping-task"}`, `rabbitmq_queue_consumers{queue="shipping-task"}`, and `rabbitmq_connections`; distinguish a publisher-side outage with no queue growth from a consumer-side outage with ready messages accumulating
2. Review `kubectl logs deployment/shipping -n <namespace> --since=30m` for `Unable to add to queue` or AMQP connection errors and `kubectl logs deployment/queue-master -n <namespace> --since=30m` for consumer connection or delivery failures; correlate the timestamps with RabbitMQ logs and the `shipping-rabbitmq` and `queue-master-rabbitmq` health results
3. Verify `kubectl get service rabbitmq -n <namespace>` and `kubectl get endpointslice -n <namespace> -l kubernetes.io/service-name=rabbitmq`; from an authorized ephemeral debug container attached to the affected application pod, test DNS and TCP connectivity to `rabbitmq:5672`, then inspect only the NetworkPolicies and fault-injection resources governing that path
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — restore RabbitMQ endpoints and TCP 5672 reachability, remove confirmed fault-injection rules, and restart only connections that do not recover. # Train Ticket Resource Faults  
## TT-RES-F5-02: Travel Service Worker Saturation from Fan-Out
- **Affected Services**: ts-travel-service, ts-basic-service, ts-route-service, ts-seat-service
- **Fault Type**: Resource
- **Symptom**: Route searches become progressively slower as concurrency rises; `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}` and `http_server_requests_seconds_bucket` increase before CPU saturation; callers receive HTTP 503 or 504 responses; traces contain parallel route, seat, and basic-info calls followed by a long join delay
- **Root Cause**: A single travel query fans out to several services and waits synchronously for every result. Under load, blocked fan-out tasks consume all request workers and connection slots in `ts-travel-service`, causing queue growth and head-of-line blocking even when individual dependencies are healthy
- **Diagnostic Steps**:
1. Graph `executor_active_threads{service="ts-travel-service"}`, `executor_queued_tasks{service="ts-travel-service"}`, and `http_client_connections_pending{service="ts-travel-service"}`; compare queue growth with incoming request concurrency rather than CPU alone
2. Inspect traces for repeated `ts-travel-service` fan-out spans and identify which child span determines the join duration; search logs for pool-acquisition timeouts using the same trace IDs
3. On one affected replica, run `kubectl exec -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> -- kill -3 1` to emit a JVM thread dump, then inspect it with `kubectl logs -n <namespace> <ts-travel-pod> --since=10m | grep -E 'Future.get|WAITING|BLOCKED|pool|timeout'`; confirm replica readiness with `kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l app=ts-travel-service`
- **Remediation & Mitigation**: Immediate — cap concurrent fan-out, reject excess work with a retryable response, and disable optional child calls while preserving the core route-search path; increase replicas only if dependencies and connection pools have verified headroom. Long-term — apply bulkheads per dependency, bounded queues, partial-result handling, cancellation propagation, and concurrency load tests that validate executor and client-pool sizing together
